Stochastic Regret Guarantees for Online Zeroth- and First-Order Bilevel Optimization
Parvin Nazari, Bojian Hou, Davoud Ataee Tarzanagh, Li Shen, George Michailidis, Los Angeles

Abstract
Online bilevel optimization (OBO) is a powerful framework for machine learning problems where both outer and inner objectives evolve over time, requiring dynamic updates. Current OBO approaches rely on deterministic window-smoothed regret minimization, which may not accurately reflect system performance when functions change rapidly. In this work, we introduce a novel search direction and show that both first- and zeroth-order (ZO) stochastic OBO algorithms leveraging this direction achieve sublinear stochastic bilevel regret without window smoothing. Beyond these guarantees, our framework enhances efficiency by: (i) reducing oracle dependence in hypergradient estimation, (ii) updating inner and outer variables alongside the linear system solution, and (iii) employing ZO-based estimation of Hessians, Jacobians, and gradients. Experiments on online parametric loss tuning and black-box adversarial attacks validate our approach.



1
Introduction

Bilevel optimization (BO) minimizes an outer objective dependent on an inner problem’s solution. Originating in game theory
stackelberg1952theory
and formalized in mathematical optimization
bracken1973mathematical
, BO finds applications in operations research, engineering, economics
dempe2002foundations
, and image processing
crockett2022bilevel
. Recently, BO has gained traction in machine learning, including hyperparameter optimization
franceschi2018bilevel
, meta-learning
finn2017model
, reinforcement learning
stadie2020learning
, and neural architecture search
liu2018darts
.

In the
offline
setting, BO solves the following problem:

{\bf{x}}^{*}\in\text{argmin}_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}f({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}({\bf{x}}))\quad\textnormal{subj.\penalty\ to}\quad{\bf{y}}^{*}({\bf{x}})=\text{argmin}_{{\bf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}g({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}),

(BO)

where
f
and
g
are the outer and inner objectives, with
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
as their respective variables.

OBO
tarzanagh2024online
addresses dynamic scenarios where objectives evolve over time, requiring the agent to update the outer decision in response to the optimal inner decision. Similar to online single-level optimization (OSO)
zinkevich2003online
, OBO involves iterative decision-making without prior knowledge of outcomes
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
;
bohne2024online
. Let
T
be the total number of rounds. Define
\mathbf{x}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
as the decision variable and
f_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as the outer function. Similarly, define
\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
and
g_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the inner problem, where

\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\text{argmin}_{{\bf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

OBO can be seen as a
single-player
problem, where the player selects
\mathbf{x}_{t}
without knowing
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
, using
\mathbf{y}_{t}
as an estimate based on
g_{t}
. Alternatively, it can be framed as a
two-player
game
stackelberg1952theory
, where the leader (
\mathbf{x}_{t}
) competes with the follower (
\mathbf{y}_{t}
), who selects
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
based on limited knowledge of
g_{t}
. This framework includes online and adversarial variants of (
BO
), such as online actor-critic algorithms
zhou2020online
, online meta-learning
finn2019online
, and online hyperparameter optimization
lin2024non
. The inner and outer functions may be time-varying, adversarial, unavailable
a priori
, and require
nonstationary
optimization.

w
, solver iterations, stochastic/constrained regrets, feedback type, and local bounds.
\kappa_{g}
denotes the condition number of the inner objective
g_{t}
.

,

,

,
\Psi_{T}
,
\hat{\Delta}_{T}
,
\hat{\Psi}_{T}
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{\sigma}
are defined in (
11
), (

), (

), (

), and (

), respectively.

Our Contributions.
This paper addresses stochastic OBO, introducing novel first- and zeroth-order methods to minimize stochastic bilevel regret. Key contributions are summarized below.

\bullet

Stochastic regret minimization without window-smoothing.

Existing OBO methods
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
;
huang2023online
;
bohne2024online
rely on deterministic
window-smoothed
regret minimization, which may not accurately reflect system performance when functions change rapidly. We address these limitations by introducing a novel search direction (Section
2
) and proving that both first-order and ZO methods achieve sublinear
stochastic bilevel regret without window-smoothing (
w=1
)
; see Theorems
2.6
and
3.2
and Table
1
.

\bullet

OBO with function value oracle feedback.

In large-scale and black-box settings
chen2017zoo
;
nesterov2005smooth
, first- and second-order information is often unavailable or costly. Constructing accurate (hyper)-gradient estimators using only function value oracles is particularly challenging due to BO’s nested structure. Existing methods rely on gradient, Hessian, and Jacobian oracles, limiting scalability
franceschi2017forward
;
ghadimi2018approximation
. We propose Algorithm
2
, which estimates Hessians, Jacobians, and gradients using function value oracles, achieving sublinear local regret (Theorem
3.2
).

\bullet

OBO with one subproblem solver iteration.

A major challenge in BO is solving implicit systems to approximate the hypergradient
ji2021bilevel
;
chen2021closing
. While efficient offline BO methods exist
ji2021bilevel
;
dagreou2022framework
, extending them to OBO is difficult due to time-varying objectives. SOBOW
lin2024non
partially addresses this using a conjugate gradient (CG) algorithm with increasing iterations (Table
1
). We improve upon SOBOW by introducing Algorithms
1
and
2
, which require only a
single
subproblem solver iteration.

2
Stochastic OBO with Access to First- and Inner Second-Order Oracles

Notation.

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional real space;
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{+}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{++}
denote its nonnegative and positive orthants. Bold lowercase letters (e.g.,
{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}
) represent vectors,
\langle{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\rangle
is the inner product, and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
is the Euclidean norm.
\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}
denotes the gradient, and
\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}=\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}
. A function is
L
-smooth if its gradient is
L
-Lipschitz. The projection onto a convex set
\mathcal{X}
is
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}({\bf{z}})=\text{argmin}_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{z}}\|^{2}
. We use
[T]
for
\{1,\ldots,T\}
,
{\mathbb{E}}[\cdot]
for expectation, and
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
to hide problem-independent constants.

Stochastic OBO Setting
. Let
T
be the total rounds
tarzanagh2024online
. Define
\mathbf{x}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
as the decision variable and
f_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
as the outer objective. The inner decision variable and objective are
\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
and
g_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, where the optimal inner decision is:

{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\in\underset{{\bf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}{\text{argmin}}\left\{g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}):=\underset{\zeta_{t}\sim\mathcal{D}_{g,t}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t})\right]\right\}.

(1)

Further, we have

f_{t}({\bf{x}},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})):=\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{t}\sim\mathcal{D}_{f,t}}\left[f_{t}({\bf{x}},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x});\xi_{t})\right].

Here,
(\mathcal{D}_{f,t},\mathcal{D}_{g,t})
denote data distributions at time
t
. Our setting is stochastic, with only noisy evaluations of functions, gradients, and Hessians. Unlike OSO
zinkevich2003online
, where true losses are revealed, in OBO the outer function
f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
is inaccessible for updating
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and is generally non-convex in
\mathbf{x}
, making standard regret notions from online convex optimization
hazan2016introduction
unsuitable.

Given a sequence
\{\alpha_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{++}\}_{t=1}^{T}
, we define the following notion of
bilevel local regret
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{BL-Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\big\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}\left({\bf{x}}_{t};\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\right)\big\|^{2}\right],\quad\textnormal{with}\qquad

(2a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}\left({\bf{x}}_{t};\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}\Big({\bf{x}}_{t}-\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\big[{\bf{x}}_{t}-\alpha_{t}\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\big]\Big).

(2b)

The local regret (
2
) compares the leader’s decision
{\bf{x}}_{t}
to the stationary points
{\bf{x}}_{t}^{*}
satisfying

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}\left({\bf{x}}_{t}^{*};\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t}^{*},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}^{*}))\right)=0.

This can also be viewed as dynamic local regret, as the baseline corresponds to a stationary point of the leader’s objective
f_{t}
.

Figure 1:
Smoothly
and
rapidly
changing
f_{t}
in OBO with
g_{t}(x_{t},y_{t})=(y_{t}-\cos(x_{t}))^{2}
,
a_{t}=1+0.5\sin(t)
,
b_{t}=1+\sin(0.5t)
, and
c_{t}=10b_{t}
.

Previous work on (nonconvex) OBO examined unconstrained local regret using window-smoothed objectives:

F_{t,w}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})=({1}/{w})\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}f_{t-i}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}).

For
w=1
and
\mathcal{X}={\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
, this reduces to (
2
).
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
showed that
w=o(T)
ensures sublinear regret under slow variations in
\{F_{t,w}\}_{t=1}^{T}
, while rapid changes can lead to deviations. However, smoothing may misrepresent regret (Figure
1
). This paper introduces a new projection-based local regret notion (
2
) without smoothing, and establishes sublinear regret for constrained OBO.

Online Gradient Descent (OGD)
.
One of the most widely used algorithms for online (single-level) optimization is OGD
zinkevich2003online
. The procedure for OGD is as follows: For each
t\in[T]
, the algorithm selects
{\bf{x}}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
, observes the function
f_{t}:\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, and updates according to

{\bf{x}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\big({\bf{x}}_{t}-\alpha_{t}\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\big),\qquad\alpha_{t}>0.

(OGD)

In the following, we adapt
OGD
to OBO and introduce a novel framework that requires limited feedback and can utilize ZO updates within a single-loop structure.

To adapt
OGD
to OBO,
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
;
bohne2024online
developed a variant alternating between inner and outer
OGD
, achieving sublinear bilevel regret bounds. We introduce a new search direction that enables sublinear bilevel regret without window smoothing. To compute the hypergradient
\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))
where
{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})
is defined in (
1
), since
\nabla_{\bf{y}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))=0
, using the implicit function theorem, yields

\displaystyle\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bf{x}}f_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\right)+\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\right){\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}),

(3)

where
{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
is the solution to the following linear system:

\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\right){\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\right)=0.

(4)

As the exact
{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})
is not available, we estimate the hypergradient of
f_{t}
at
({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
and introduce
an auxiliary variable
{\bf{v}}:={\bf{v}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
to effectively decouple
the nonlinear structure in
\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}))
, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{\bf{x}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})+\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){\bf{v}}_{t},

(5a)

where
{\bf{v}}_{t}
serves as an inexact solution to the
linear system

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){\bf{v}}_{t}+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})=0.

(5b)

An accurate solution of (
5b
) is crucial for tight regret bounds.
tarzanagh2024online
assumes an exact solution, which is restrictive in large-scale settings. To address this,
lin2024non
proposed an efficient OBO algorithm with window averaging, using CG methods to solve (
5b
), which is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\vskip-5.69046pt\text{min}_{{\bf{v}}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){\bf{v}}_{t}+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})\right\|^{2}.\vskip-5.69046pt

(6)

Next, we introduce a novel search direction that enables both first- and ZO stochastic OBO algorithms to achieve sublinear bilevel regret without smoothing. We first state the following lemma:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
w=t
,
W=1/\eta
and
\nu=1-\eta
for
\eta\in(0,1)
in the window-smoothed gradient

{\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}\nu^{i}{\nabla}f_{t-i}({\bf{x}}_{t-i},{\bf{y}}_{t-i};\mathcal{B}_{t-i})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{t}:=\{\xi_{t,1},\ldots,\xi_{t,b}\}
is drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}_{f,t}
. Then,
{\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\eta(1-\eta)^{t-j}{\nabla}f_{j}({\bf{x}}_{j},{\bf{y}}_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j})
, and we have
{\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})={{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}
with
{{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}=\eta{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-\eta){{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}
, and
{{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{1}=\frac{1}{W}{\nabla}f_{1}({\bf{x}}_{1},{\bf{y}}_{1};\mathcal{B}_{1})
for all
t\geq 2
.

Proof is given in Appendix
C.1
.
As shown in Lemma
2.1
, for a specific choice of
w
and
W
, the time-smoothed gradient forms a recursive momentum-type search direction. However, achieving sublinear regret in stochastic OBO requires large-window smoothing (
w=o(T)
)
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
;
bohne2024online
. To address this, we propose the following search direction:

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}=\eta{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-\eta){\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}+(1-\eta)({\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})-{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t-1},{\bf{y}}_{t-1};\mathcal{B}_{t})).

(7)

This direction is used for updating
{\bf{x}}
, with similar updates for
{\bf{y}}
and
{\bf{v}}
, as discussed below and detailed in Algorithm
1
. The quadratic formulation of (
5b
) in (
6
) motivates single-loop methods such as
dagreou2022framework
. Building on this, we propose Simultaneous Online Gradient Descent (SOGD) for constrained OBO, presented in Algorithm
1
. At each step, SOGD jointly updates the follower variable
{\bf{y}}_{t}
, auxiliary variable
{\bf{v}}_{t}
, and leader variable
{\bf{x}}_{t}
using batches
\mathcal{B}_{t}=\{\xi_{t,1},\ldots,\xi_{t,b}\}
and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}:=\{\zeta_{t,1},\ldots,\zeta_{t,\bar{b}}\}
sampled i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}_{f,t}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g,t}
. Step
S1.
only requires computing Hessian-vector products, avoiding explicit computation of
\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}
or
\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}
. Step
S2.
uses the projection:

\displaystyle{\Pi}_{\mathcal{Z}_{p}}(\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\text{argmin}_{{\bf{z}}\in\mathcal{Z}_{p}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bf{v}}-{\bf{z}}\|^{2}=\min\left\{1,\frac{p}{\|\mathbf{v}\|}\right\}\mathbf{v},\quad\textnormal{where}\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{p}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\mid\|\mathbf{v}\|\leq p\right\}.

(8)

Unlike OAGD
tarzanagh2024online
with alternating loops, and SOBOW
lin2024non
using CG, SOGD performs a single OGD step for all variables.

Algorithm 1

SOGD

0:

({\bf{x}}_{1},{\bf{y}}_{1},{\bf{v}}_{1})\in\mathcal{X}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{p}
;

p\in\mathbb{R}_{++}
;
T\in{\mathbb{N}}
;
stepsizes
\{(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\delta_{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{++}^{3}\}_{t=1}^{T}
;
parameters
\{(\gamma_{t},\lambda_{t},\eta_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}\in(0,1)
;

{\bf{z}}_{t}:=({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}).

For

t=1

to

T

do
:

S1.

Draw samples
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
with batch sizes
b
and
\bar{b}
. Get search directions
{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t},{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{v}}}_{t}
, and
{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}
:

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})=\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}),

(9a)

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t}={\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})+(1-\gamma_{t})({\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t-1}-{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})),

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{vv}}}_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)=\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right){\bf{v}}_{t},

(9b)

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{v}}}_{t}={\bf{d}}^{{\bf{v}}{\bf{v}}}_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)+(1-\lambda_{t})({\bf{d}}^{{\bf{v}}}_{t-1}-{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{v}}{\bf{v}}}_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\mathcal{B}_{t})),

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{xx}}}_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)=\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right){\bf{v}}_{t},

(9c)

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}={\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}{\bf{x}}}_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)+(1-\eta_{t})({\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}-{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{x}}{\bf{x}}}_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\mathcal{B}_{t})).

S2.

Update inner, system, and outer solutions:

\displaystyle\vskip-10.0pt{\bf{y}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\bf{y}}_{t}-\beta_{t}{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\bf{v}}_{t+1}={\Pi}_{\mathcal{Z}_{p}}\big[{\bf{v}}_{t}-\delta_{t}{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}\big],\quad{\bf{x}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}[{\bf{x}}_{t}-\alpha_{t}{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}}].\vskip-15.0pt

Assumption 2.2
.

g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
is twice continuously differentiable and
\mu_{g}
-strongly convex in
{\bf{y}}
for all
{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X},t\in[T]
.

Assumption 2.3
.

Let
{\bf{z}}=[{\bf{x}};{\bf{y}}]
and
{\bf{z}}^{\prime}=[{\bf{x}}^{\prime};{\bf{y}}^{\prime}]
, where
{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and
{\bf{y}},{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}
. For any
{\bf{z}}
,
{\bf{z}}^{\prime}
, and
t\in[T]
:

B1.

\exists\penalty\ \ell_{f,0}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
s.t.
\|f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-f_{t}({\bf{z}}^{\prime};\xi_{t})\|\leq\ell_{f,0}\|{\bf{z}}-{\bf{z}}^{\prime}\|
;

B2.

\exists\penalty\ \ell_{f,1}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
s.t.
\|\nabla f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-\nabla f_{t}({\bf{z}}^{\prime};\xi_{t})\|\leq\ell_{f,1}\|{\bf{z}}-{\bf{z}}^{\prime}\|
;

B3.

\exists\penalty\ \ell_{g,1}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
s.t.
\|\nabla g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla g_{t}({\bf{z}}^{\prime};\zeta_{t})\|\leq\ell_{g,1}\|{\bf{z}}-{\bf{z}}^{\prime}\|
;

B4.

\exists\penalty\ \ell_{g,2}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
s.t.
\|\nabla^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}}^{\prime};\zeta_{t})\|\leq\ell_{g,2}\|{\bf{z}}-{\bf{z}}^{\prime}\|
.

Assumption 2.4
.

For any
t\in[T]
,
|f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))|\leq M
for some
M\in{\mathbb{R}}_{++}
and any
{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Assumption 2.5
.

There exist constants
\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}},\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}},\sigma_{g_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}},\sigma_{f_{{\bf{y}}}},\sigma_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}
such that, for all
{\bf{z}}=[{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}]
:

C1.

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}
;

C2.

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}}^{2}
;

C3.

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{g_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}}^{2}
;

C4.

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{f_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}
;

C5.

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\sigma_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2}
.

Throughout this paper, we define

\sigma^{2}:=\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\sigma_{f_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\sigma_{g_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\sigma_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2}.

(10)

Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
are standard in BO
chen2021closing
;
ji2021bilevel
and OBO
tarzanagh2024online
, and hold for many bilevel ML problems
franceschi2018bilevel
. Assumption
2.4
is typical in non-convex OSO
hazan2017efficient
;
lin2024non
, while Assumption
2.5
assumes unbiased stochastic gradient, Hessian, and Jacobian estimators with bounded variance
chen2021closing
.

Achieving sublinear dynamic regret is generally infeasible under arbitrary time variations
besbes2015non
. Prior analyses
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
bound regret by enforcing regularity on the comparator sequence. To attain sublinear regret,
tarzanagh2024online
introduces the following regularity metrics for bilevel sequences:

\begin{split}H_{p,T}&:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}\|{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t-1}({\bf{x}})-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\|^{p},\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptV_{T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}\left|f_{t-1}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t-1}({\bf{x}}))-f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))\right|.\end{split}

(11)

Path-length
H_{p,T}
measures changes in the follower’s costs, while
V_{T}
captures the leader’s objective smoothness. We use path-length for the follower and function variation for the leader due to the follower’s strong convexity (Assumption
2.2
) versus the leader’s nonconvexity. Another regularity is the sequential gradient difference of the outer objective:

\displaystyle D_{{\bf{x}},T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}}\left\|\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t-1}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})\right\|^{2},

(12a)

\displaystyle D_{{\bf{y}},T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}}\left\|\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t-1}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})\right\|^{2}.

(12b)

As in
huangonline
;
hallak2021regret
,
D_{{\bf{x}},T}
and
D_{{\bf{y}},T}
measure the gradient drift of
f_{t}
relative to
f_{t-1}
for
{\bf{x}}
and
{\bf{y}}
, respectively. We define deviations in the gradient, Hessian, and Jacobian of the inner objective as:

\displaystyle G_{{\bf{y}},T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\|\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t-1}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2},\hskip 9.24994ptG_{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}},T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\|\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t-1}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2},

\displaystyle G_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}},T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\|\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t-1}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}.

(13)

We introduce the following notations for simplicity:

\begin{split}\Delta_{T}&:=E_{1}+V_{T},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Psi_{T}:=H_{2,T}+G_{T}+D_{T},\end{split}

(14)

where
(V_{T},H_{p,T})
are defined in (
11
), and

\begin{split}E_{1}&:=\|{\bf{y}}_{1}-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{1}({\bf{x}}_{1})\|^{2}+\|{\bf{v}}_{1}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{1}({\bf{x}}_{1})\|^{2},\quad G_{T}:=G_{{\bf{y}},T}+G_{{\bf{y}}{\bf{y}},T}+G_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}},T},\\
D_{T}&:=D_{{\bf{x}},T}+D_{{\bf{y}},T}.\end{split}

(15)

By accounting for both
D_{T}
and
G_{T}
, we can represent the variations in the environments of OBO.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\{(f_{t},g_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
be the sequence of functions presented to Algorithm
1
, satisfying Assumptions
2.2
-
2.5
.
For all
t\in[T]
, let

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\frac{1}{(c+t)^{1/3}},\quad\beta_{t}=c_{\beta}\alpha_{t},\quad\delta_{t}=c_{\delta}\alpha_{t},\quad b=\bar{b}=1,

\displaystyle\gamma_{t+1}=c_{\gamma}\alpha^{2}_{t},\quad\eta_{t+1}=c_{\eta}\alpha^{2}_{t},\quad\lambda_{t+1}=c_{\lambda}\alpha^{2}_{t}.

(16)

Here,
c
,
c_{\beta}
,
c_{\delta}
,
c_{\gamma}
,
c_{\eta}
, and
c_{\lambda}
are specified in (
109
). Algorithm
1
guarantees:

\displaystyle\textnormal{BL-Reg}_{T}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(T^{\frac{1}{3}}(\sigma^{2}+\Delta_{T})+T^{\frac{2}{3}}\Psi_{T}\right),

(17)

where
\sigma
and
(\Delta_{T},\Psi_{T})
are defined in (
10
) and (
14
).

Remark 2.7

(
Stochastic Regret Guarantee for OBO and OSO with
w=1
)
.

Theorem
2.6
bounds the regret of Algorithm
1
without window-smoothing, based on the regularities in (
14
). We note that the average dynamic regret

\textnormal{BL-Reg}_{T}/T\leq\mathcal{O}(T^{-2/3}(\sigma^{2}+\Delta_{T})+T^{-1/3}\Psi_{T})

remains sublinear under suitable conditions on
\Delta_{T}
,
\Psi_{T}
, and
\sigma
. Specifically, if
\Delta_{T}=o(T^{2/3})
,
\Psi_{T}=o(T^{1/3})
, and
\sigma=o(T^{1/3})
, then the dynamic regret grows sublinearly, i.e., BL-Reg
{}_{T}=o(T)
; see Appendix
B.2
for further examples and discussion. This result also yields a sharper
T^{-2/3}\sigma^{2}
regret—improving over the
T^{-1/2}\sigma^{2}
bound for stochastic OBO
bohne2024online
—and removes the need for window-smoothing
bohne2024online
;
tarzanagh2024online
;
lin2024non
;
huang2023online
. For OSO, this result surpasses the
T^{-1/2}\sigma^{2}
rate in
hallak2021regret
.

3
Stochastic OBO with Zeroth-Order Oracles

Black-box optimization arises when gradients are unavailable
chen2017zoo
. We study ZO-OBO methods with limited access to leader and follower objectives. Let
{\bf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
{\bf{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
be vectors uniformly sampled from unit balls
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
. Given smoothing parameters
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{{\bf{s}}},\rho_{{\bf{r}}})
, we define Gaussian-smoothed objectives using
nesterov2017random
:

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046ptf_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left(\mathbf{x},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{({\bf{s}},{\bf{r}},\xi_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_{t}({\bf{x}}+\rho_{{\bf{s}}}{\bf{s}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})+\rho_{{\bf{r}}}{\bf{r}};\xi_{t})\right],\quad\textnormal{where}

(18)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\in\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}{\arg\min}\big\{g_{t,\bm{\rho}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\underset{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r},\zeta_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{t}(\mathbf{x}+\rho_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{s},\mathbf{y}+\rho_{\mathbf{r}}\mathbf{r};\zeta_{t})\right]\big\}.

(19)

To solve stochastic OBO with (
18
), we need to obtain the hyper-gradient of
f_{t,\bm{\rho}}
in (
18
) at
({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
as

\displaystyle\nabla f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})):=\nabla_{\bf{x}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}))+\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})\right)\hat{{\bf{v}}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf{v}}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})\text{ is the solution to }\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})\right)\hat{{\bf{v}}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}))=0.

(20)

Obtaining
\hat{\mathbf{y}}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})
in closed-form is usually a challenging task, so it is natural to use the following gradient
surrogate. At any
({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
, we introduce an auxiliary variable
{\bf{v}}={\bf{v}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
and define:

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}):=\nabla_{\bf{x}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})+\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){{\bf{v}}},\quad\text{where}

(21a)

\displaystyle{{\bf{v}}}\text{ is the solution to }\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){{\bf{v}}}+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})=0.

(21b)

To do so, we also introduce
{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{\bf{y}}
,
{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{\bf{v}}
and
{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{{\bf{x}}}
as follows:

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{\bf{y}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})=\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}),

(22a)

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{\bf{v}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}},{\bf{v}})=\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})+\nabla_{\bf{y}}^{2}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){\bf{v}},

(22b)

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t,\bm{\rho}}^{{\bf{x}}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}},{\bf{v}})=\nabla_{\bf{x}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})+\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\right){\bf{v}}.

(22c)

Next, we approximate these directions using stochastic zeroth-order oracles (
SZO
), which produce the quantities
\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t})
,
\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t})
,
\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t})
, and
\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t})
. These are unbiased estimators of the true gradients
\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
,
\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
,
\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
, and
\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
, respectively, as shown in
flaxman2004online
, such that the following assumption holds:

\displaystyle\underset{({\bf{r}},\xi_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t})\right]={\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}),\quad\underset{({\bf{s}},\xi_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t})\right]={\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}),

\displaystyle\underset{({\bf{r}},\zeta_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t})\right]={\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}),\quad\underset{({\bf{s}},\zeta_{t})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t})\right]={\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}).

(23)

Specifically, following
shamir2017optimal
, we estimate the gradient of a function
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, querying at
{\bf{x}}-\lambda{\bf{s}}
and
{\bf{x}}+\lambda{\bf{s}}
, yielding an estimator

(d/2\lambda)\left(h({\bf{x}}+\lambda{\bf{s}})-h({\bf{x}}-\lambda{\bf{s}})\right){\bf{s}}.

Using this strategy, the finite-difference estimation of
\nabla g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
, denoted by
\hat{\nabla}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}})
, is constructed for given smoothing parameters
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{{\bf{s}}},\rho_{{\bf{r}}})
, and a set
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}=\{\zeta_{t,1},\ldots,\zeta_{t,\bar{b}}\}
drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}_{g,t}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{d_{2}}{2\bar{b}\rho_{{\bf{r}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{b}}\left(g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}+\rho_{{\bf{r}}}{\bf{r}}_{i};\zeta_{t,i})-g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}-\rho_{{\bf{r}}}{\bf{r}}_{i};\zeta_{t,i})\right){\bf{r}}_{i},

(24a)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{d_{1}}{2\bar{b}\rho_{{\bf{s}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{b}}\left(g_{t}({\bf{x}}+\rho_{{\bf{s}}}{\bf{s}}_{i},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t,i})-g_{t}({\bf{x}}-\rho_{{\bf{s}}}{\bf{s}}_{i},{\bf{y}};\zeta_{t,i})\right){\bf{s}}_{i}.

(24b)

Similarly, we estimate
{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\mathcal{B}_{t})
and
{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\mathcal{B}_{t})
, respectively, using a batch
\mathcal{B}_{t}=\{\xi_{t,1},\ldots,\xi_{t,b}\}
drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{D}_{f,t}
, by

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\mathcal{B}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{d_{2}}{2b\rho_{{\bf{r}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{b}(f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}+\rho_{{\bf{r}}}{\bf{r}}_{i};\xi_{t,i})-f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}-\rho_{{\bf{r}}}{\bf{r}}_{i};\xi_{t,i})){\bf{r}}_{i},

(25a)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\mathcal{B}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{d_{1}}{2b\rho_{{\bf{s}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{b}(f_{t}({\bf{x}}+\rho_{{\bf{s}}}{\bf{s}}_{i},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t,i})-f_{t}({\bf{x}}-\rho_{{\bf{s}}}{\bf{s}}_{i},{\bf{y}};\xi_{t,i}){\bf{s}}_{i}.

(25b)

Furthermore, given a smoothing parameter
\rho_{{\bf{v}}}>0
, we approximate the Hessian-vector product
\nabla_{\bf{y}}^{2}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}){\bf{v}}
and the Jacobian-vector product
\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}){\bf{v}}
as the finite difference between two gradients, respectively, as

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\bar{b}\rho_{{\bf{v}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{b}}(\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}+\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}};\zeta_{t,i})-\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}-\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}};\zeta_{t,i})),

(26a)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\bar{b}\rho_{{\bf{v}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\bar{b}}(\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}+\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}};\zeta_{t,i})-\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}-\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}};\zeta_{t,i})).

(26b)

Algorithm 2

ZO-SOGD

0:

In addition to parameters in SOGD, choose
\rho_{{\bf{v}}},\rho_{{\bf{r}}},\rho_{{\bf{s}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{++}
.

For

t=1

to

T

do
:

S1.

Draw samples
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
with batch sizes
b
and
\bar{b}
.
Using (
24
)–(
26
), get:

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right)=\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}),

(27a)

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}={\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})+(1-\gamma_{t})(\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t-1}^{{\bf{y}}}-{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t})),

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}{\bf{v}}}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)=\hat{\nabla}_{\bf{y}}f_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)+\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right),

(27b)

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}={\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}{\bf{v}}}({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-{\lambda}_{t})(\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t-1}^{{\bf{v}}}-{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}{\bf{v}}}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\mathcal{B}_{t})),

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)=\hat{\nabla}_{\bf{x}}f_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t}\right)+\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right),

(27c)

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}}={\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}({\bf{z}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-{\eta}_{t})(\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t-1}^{{\bf{x}}}-{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}({\bf{z}}_{t-1};\mathcal{B}_{t})),

S2.

Update inner, system, and outer solutions:

\displaystyle\vskip-10.0pt{\bf{y}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\bf{y}}_{t}-{\beta_{t}}\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ {\bf{v}}_{t+1}={\Pi}_{\mathcal{Z}_{p}}\big[{\bf{v}}_{t}-\delta_{t}\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}\big],\quad{\bf{x}}_{t+1}=\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\big[{\bf{x}}_{t}-\alpha_{t}\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}}\big].\vskip-15.0pt

Using (
24
)–(
26
), the first-order terms in (
9
) are approximated by
\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}
,
\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}
, and
\hat{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}}
in (
27
). The approximations in (
26a
) and (
26b
) introduce errors in the hypergradient, which must be controlled. (
26
) depends on the dimension of
{\bf{y}}
, as in ZO optimization
nesterov2017random
;
shamir2017optimal
. The projection
{\Pi}_{\mathcal{Z}_{p}}
in (
2
) bounds
{\bf{v}}
, controlling variance in
{\bf{v}}
and
{\bf{x}}
updates for convergence.

Assumption 3.1
.

There exist constants
\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{y}}}},\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{x}}}},\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{y}}}},\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}
such that, for all
{\bf{z}}=[{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}]
:

D1.

{\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2},

D2.

{\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}};\zeta_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2},

D3.

{\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2},

D4.

{\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{\nabla}_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}};\xi_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t,\bm{\rho}}({\bf{z}})\|^{2}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2}.

Assumption
3.1
is analogous to the upper bound on the variance of stochastic partial gradients discussed in
luo2020stochastic
;
wang2020zeroth
.
We simplify the notation by introducing the following shorthand.

\begin{split}\hat{\sigma}^{2}:=\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{g_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}+\hat{\sigma}_{f_{{\bf{x}}}}^{2}.\end{split}

(28)

Next, we establish a regret bound for ZO-SOGD. Similar to the previous results, we introduce regularity conditions for the smoothed functions defined in (
18
) and (
19
).

Inner Gradient Variations
: In ZO setting, we use a set of gradient variations at the perturbed point as follows:

G_{{\bf{v}},T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}(\chi_{1t}+\chi_{2t}),\qquad\quad G_{{\bf{x}},T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}(\chi_{3t}+\chi_{4t}),

(29)

where

{\bf{z}}_{t}^{+}:=({\bf{x}}_{t-1},{\bf{y}}_{t-1}+\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}}_{t-1})
,
{\bf{z}}_{t}^{-}:=({\bf{x}}_{t-1},{\bf{y}}_{t-1}-\rho_{{\bf{v}}}{\bf{v}}_{t-1})
, and

\displaystyle\chi_{1t}

\displaystyle:=\|\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{+})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t-1}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{+})\|^{2},\quad\chi_{2t}:=\|\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{-})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t-1}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{-})\|^{2},

\displaystyle\chi_{3t}

\displaystyle:=\|\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{+})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t-1}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{+})\|^{2},\quad\chi_{4t}:=\|\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{-})-\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}g_{t-1}({\bf{z}}_{t}^{-})\|^{2}.

Further, for simplicity of notation, we define

\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}_{T}:=E_{1}+V_{T}+D_{T}+G_{{\bf{y}},T},\quad\quad\hat{\Psi}_{T}:=H_{2,T}+G_{{\bf{v}},T}+G_{{\bf{x}},T},\end{split}

(30)

where
(V_{T},H_{p,T})
and
(E_{1},D_{T})
are defined in (
11
), and (
15
), respectively. Moreover,
G_{{\bf{y}},T}
and
(G_{{\bf{v}},T},G_{{\bf{x}},T})
are defined in (
2
) and (
29
), respectively.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\{(f_{t},g_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
be the sequence of functions presented to Algorithm
2
, satisfying Assumptions
2.2
-
2.4
and
3.1
. For all
t\in[T]
, let

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\frac{1}{(d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/4}(c+t)^{1/3}},\quad\beta_{t}=c_{\beta}\alpha_{t},\quad\delta_{t}=c_{\delta}\alpha_{t},\quad\gamma_{t+1}=c_{\gamma}\alpha_{t},

\displaystyle\eta_{t+1}=c_{\eta}\alpha_{t},\quad\lambda_{t+1}=c_{\lambda}\alpha_{t},\quad\rho_{{\bf{v}}}^{2}=c_{{\bf{v}}}\alpha_{t},\quad\rho_{{\bf{r}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{d_{2}^{2}T},\quad\rho_{{\bf{s}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{d_{1}^{2}T},

\displaystyle b=\frac{T^{1/3}}{(d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/2}},\quad\bar{b}=\frac{T^{2/3}}{(d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/4}},

(31)

where
c
,
c_{\beta}
,
c_{\delta}
,
c_{\gamma}
,
c_{\eta}
,
c_{{\bf{v}}}
, and
c_{\lambda}
are specified in (
228
). Let
p=\ell_{f,0}/\mu_{g}
for the set
\mathcal{Z}_{p}
defined in (
2
). Then, Algorithm
2
guarantees:

\displaystyle\textnormal{BL-Reg}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left((d_{1}+d_{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}T^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\hat{\sigma}^{2}+\hat{\Delta}_{T}\right)\right.\left.+(d_{1}+d_{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}\hat{\Psi}_{T}\right).

where
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
and
(\hat{\Delta}_{T},\hat{\Psi}_{T})
are defined in (
28
) and (
30
).

Theorem
3.2
bounds the regret of Algorithm
2
without window-smoothing, based on the regularities in (
30
). We note that the average dynamic regret
\textnormal{BL-Reg}_{T}/T\leq\mathcal{O}((d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/4}T^{-2/3}\left(\hat{\sigma}^{2}+\hat{\Delta}_{T}\right)+(d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/2}T^{-1/3}\hat{\Psi}_{T})

remains sublinear under suitable conditions on
\hat{\Delta}_{T}
,
\hat{\Psi}_{T}
, and
\hat{\sigma}
.

Remark 3.3

(
Regret Guarantee for Zeroth Order OBO
)
.

Theorem
3.2
provides the first regret guarantee for OBO with access only to noisy function evaluations of the leader and follower. The dimensional dependence
\mathcal{O}(d_{1}+d_{2})
in Theorem
3.2
aligns with optimal results for simpler offline min-max problems
huang2022accelerated
. The bound also depends on the sample sizes
b,\bar{b}
and smoothing parameters
\rho_{{\bf{v}}},\rho_{{\bf{r}}},\rho_{{\bf{s}}}
at each iteration.

Remark 3.4

(
Improved Regret for OSO
)
.

Our dynamic regret for single-level non-stationary optimization is
\mathcal{O}((d_{1}+d_{2})^{3/4}T^{-2/3}(\hat{\sigma}^{2}+E_{1}+V_{T}+D_{T}))
, improving the result in
roy2022stochastic
, which is
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2}\sigma^{2}\sqrt{d})
.
roy2022stochastic
proposed a zeroth-order stochastic gradient descent algorithm for unconstrained, non-convex, time-varying objective functions, achieving a regret bound of
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2}\sigma^{2}\sqrt{dW_{T}})
using a two-point gradient estimator, where
W_{T}
bounds the nonstationarity. Additionally,
guan2023hardness
showed that the local regret for standard online stochastic gradient descent with the standard two-point gradient estimator
agarwal2010optimal
is
\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2}d\sqrt{V_{T}})
.

4
Experimental Results

In this section, we present experimental results for two applications: online black-box attacks on deep neural networks and parametric loss tuning for imbalanced data. Code is available at

. Additional experiments and details on hyperparameter tuning are provided in Appendix
E
.

Bilevel Optimization for Black-Box Adversarial Attacks (BBAA)

Deep neural networks are vulnerable to adversarial examples—inputs subtly perturbed to mislead classifiers. These examples can fool models without access to their internals, as in
chen2017zoo
;
liu2018zeroth
;
chen2019zo
. We first review the ZO single-level formulation for BBAA
chen2017zoo
. Let
(\mathbf{a},b)
be a clean image
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with label
b\in\{1,\ldots,J\}
, and define
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{y}
, where
\mathbf{y}
is the adversarial perturbation. Let
\mathcal{Y}:=[-5,5]^{d}
, and
\ell:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the black-box attack loss. For a given hyperparameter
\lambda>0
, the BBAA problem is:

\min_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}}\;\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell(\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{y})+\lambda\|\mathbf{y}\|^{2}.

(32)

To adapt (
32
) to our OBO, consider OBO for supervised learning: at each timestep
t
, new samples
({\bf{a}}_{t},b_{t})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}:=\{\mathcal{D}^{\text{val}}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{t}\}
are received, where
{\bf{a}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
is the feature vector (image) and
b_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
is the corresponding target. Note that the correct decision can change abruptly. We consider an
S
-stage scenario where
({\bf{x}}_{s}^{*},{\bf{y}}_{s}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{s}^{*}))
represents the best decisions for the
s
-th stage, for all
s\in[S]
:

\displaystyle{\bf{x}}^{*}_{s}\in\underset{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}{\textnormal{argmin}}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{s}}f\left({\bf{y}}^{*}_{s}({\bf{x}});\mathcal{D}^{\text{val}}_{t}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt{\bf{y}}^{*}_{s}({\bf{x}})\in\underset{{\bf{y}}\in\mathcal{Y}}{\textnormal{argmin}}\penalty\ \sum_{t=1}^{T_{s}}g\left({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}};\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{t}\right)

(33)

\displaystyle g({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{tr}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{tr}}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{tr}}}\ell({\bf{a}}^{(i)}_{t}+{\bf{y}}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\iota=1}^{p}e^{[{\bf{x}}_{t}]_{\iota}}[{\bf{y}}_{t}]_{\iota}^{2},

\displaystyle f({\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t});\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{val}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{val}}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{val}}}\ell({\bf{a}}^{(i)}_{t}+{\bf{y}}_{t}).

(34a)

Here,
\{{\bf{a}}^{(i)}_{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{tr}}}
and
\{{\bf{a}}^{(i)}_{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\text{val}}}
are batches of training and validation samples at timestep
t
;
{\bf{a}}^{(i)}_{t}
is the
i
th sample in that batch; and
[{\bf{x}}_{t}]_{\iota}
and
[{\bf{y}}_{t}]_{\iota}
denote the
\iota
th component of
{\bf{x}}_{t}
and
{\bf{y}}_{t}
, respectively.

We normalize the pixel values to
\mathcal{Y}
. For an untargeted attack, the loss in (
34
) is
\ell({\bf{a}}^{\prime}_{t})=\max\{Z({\bf{a}}^{\prime}_{t})_{b_{t}}-\max_{j\neq b_{t}}Z({\bf{a}}^{\prime}_{t})_{j},-\kappa\}
, where
Z({\bf{a}}^{\prime}_{t})_{j}
is the prediction score for class
j
given input
{\bf{a}}^{\prime}_{t}={\bf{a}}_{t}+{\bf{y}}_{t}
, and
\kappa>0
controls the confidence gap. In our experiments, we set
\kappa=0
. Eq. (
33
) introduces the first OBO formulation of BBAA. Using a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}
for hyperparameters instead of
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{++}
in (
32
) enables finer control over model components, enhancing performance for complex models and heterogeneous data
lorraine2020optimizing
. For a fair comparison with single-level BBAA, we replace
\lambda
with a fixed vector multiplied by each component of
\mathbf{y}
in (
32
). We compare our ZO-SOGD and ZO-SOGD (Adam) with the following competing methods in the online setting:
ZO-O-GD
, a single-level method that updates
{\bf{y}}_{t}
with a fixed
{\bf{x}}
at each timestep using ZO gradient descent
nesterov2017random
;
ZO-O-Adam
, a single-level method that updates
{\bf{y}}_{t}
with a fixed
{\bf{x}}
at each timestep using ZO Adam
kingma2014adam
;
chen2019zo
;
ZO-O-SignSGD
, a single-level method that updates
{\bf{y}}_{t}
with a fixed
{\bf{x}}
at each timestep using ZO SignSGD
bernstein2018signsgd
; and
ZO-O-ConservSGD
, a single-level method that updates
{\bf{y}}_{t}
with a fixed
{\bf{x}}
at each timestep using ZO Conservative SGD
kim2021curvature
. Note that ZO-SOGD (ours, Adam) is a variant of our algorithm with an adaptive stepsize, similar to that of
kingma2014adam
.

Figure 2:
Performance comparison (mean
\pm
std) of optimizers including ZO-O-GD, ZO-O-Adam, ZO-O-SignSGD, ZO-O-ConservSGD, ZO-SOGD, and ZO-SOGD (Adam) on
online adversarial attack
for MNIST data across five runs.

Figure 3:
Performance (mean
\pm
std) on
online parametric loss tuning
with distribution shift on MNIST across five runs, comparing OGD
zinkevich2003online
, OAGD
tarzanagh2024online
, SOBOW
lin2024non
, and our SOGD.

We evaluated the proposed algorithms based on runtime, test accuracy on perturbed samples, and the infinity norm of
{\bf{y}}_{t}
. Figure
2
compares the methods. The left panel shows that ZO-SOGD has a slower runtime than single-level baselines due to outer-level optimization on
{\bf{x}}
. The middle panel illustrates that accuracy decreases as the adversarial attack
{\bf{y}}
strengthens, with ZO-SOGD outperforming ZO-O-GD and ZO-O-ConservGD, while ZO-SOGD (Adam) surpasses ZO-O-Adam and all baselines. The right panel indicates that the infinity norm of
{\bf{y}}_{t}
increases over time for all methods, reducing accuracy. However, perturbations remain minor, with
\max{\bf{y}}_{t}
not exceeding 4, demonstrating that ZO-SOGD achieves effective attacks with superior performance.

Parametric Loss Tuning for
Imbalanced Data

Imbalanced datasets are common in modern machine learning, causing challenges in generalization and fairness due to underrepresented classes and sensitive attributes. Deep NNs often overfit, seeming accurate and fair during training but performing poorly during testing. A common solution is designing a parametric training loss that balances accuracy and fairness while preventing overfitting
li2021autobalance
. We consider an optimization problem similar to that in (
33
). For a new sample
(\mathbf{a}_{t},b_{t})
, the follower and leader incur a parametric and balanced cross-entropy loss, respectively:

\displaystyle g({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{t})=-\log\frac{e^{\gamma_{b_{t}}[{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{b_{t}}+\Delta_{b_{t}}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}e^{\gamma_{j}[{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{j}+\Delta_{j}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ f({\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t});\mathcal{D}^{\text{val}}_{t})=-u_{b_{t}}\log\frac{e^{[{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{b_{t}}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}e^{[{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{j}}}.

(35)

Here,
{\bf{x}}_{t}:=(\Delta_{j},\gamma_{j})_{j=1}^{J}
represents the logit adjustments, with
j
indexing the
J
classes, and
u_{j}
is the reciprocal of the proportion of samples from the
j
-th class to the total number of samples
li2021autobalance
.

In (
35
),
{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})
is the follower conditioned on the leader, and
[{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{b_{t}}
is the logit for class
b_{t}
on sample
{\bf{a}}_{t}
. The follower
{\bf{y}}_{t}
uses a 4-layer CNN, inducing a nonconvex bilevel objective. We compare SOGD with
OAGD

tarzanagh2024online
, a static method using the Neumann series, and
SOBOW

lin2024non
, a dynamic method using conjugate gradients (CG). Experiments were conducted on MNIST
lecun2010mnist
with batch size 64. We evaluated cumulative runtime, test accuracy, and balanced accuracy, defined as
\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbb{P}_{{\bf{a}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{D}_{j}}\left[\text{argmax}_{i}([{\bf{y}}_{t}({\bf{a}}_{t})]_{i})=j\right]
, where
\mathcal{D}_{j}
is the class-
j
sample distribution
li2021autobalance
. Learning rates were tuned as
\beta_{t}=\delta_{t}=\beta\in\{0.001,0.005,0.01,0.05,0.1\}
,
\alpha_{t}=\alpha\in\{0.0001,0.0005,0.001,0.005,0.01\}
, and
\gamma_{t}=\lambda_{t}=\eta_{t}=\gamma\in\{0.9,0.99,0.999\}
. Both OAGD and SOBOW used 5 iterations for their respective system solvers.

We evaluated performance over 400 timesteps in four 100-timestep phases, transitioning from an imbalanced (
0.4^{i}
) to a balanced (
0.8^{i}
) distribution for each class (
i=0,1,\ldots,9
). Figure
3
(left) shows SOBOW’s longer runtime due to CG complexity, while SOGD is the fastest with simultaneous updates. Figures
3
(middle, right) show accuracy gains as balance increases, with SOGD achieving competitive accuracy.

5
Conclusion

This work introduced a novel online bilevel optimization framework that overcomes the limitations of existing algorithms, which often depend on extensive oracle information and incur high computational costs. Our method leverages limited feedback and zeroth-order updates for efficient hypergradient estimation and simultaneous updates of decision variables, achieving
sublinear
bilevel regret without window smoothing. Experiments on online parametric loss tuning and black-box adversarial attacks validate its effectiveness. A limitation of this study is that the results focus on nonconvex regret bounds, without extending guarantees to convex settings.

We thank the reviewers for their valuable comments.
The work of DAT was supported by Samsung SDS Research America, Mountain View.
The work of GM was supported in part by NSF grants DMS–2348640 and DMS–2319552.

Appendix A
Related Work

BO
was introduced in game theory by
[
66
]
and modeled mathematically in
[
10
]
. Initial works
[
33
,
56
]
reduced it to single-level optimization. Recently, gradient-based approaches have gained popularity for their simplicity and efficacy
[
22
,
28
,
44
,
13
,
57
,
13
,
5
,
64
]
, though they assume offline objectives.

OBO
was initiated by
[
69
]
, proposing the OAGD method with regret bounds.
[
41
]
developed algorithms for online minimax optimization, special cases of OBO with local regret guarantees.
[
51
]
introduced SOBOW, a single-loop optimizer using window-smoothed functions and multiple CGs for nonconvex-strongly-convex cases. Unlike these works, we propose using
projected gradient
as a more general performance measure for constrained objectives, focusing on the original functions and their regret; See Table
1
for a comparison.

Single-Level Regret Minimization.

Single-level online optimization predominantly focuses on convex problems, either with static or dynamic convex regret minimization
[
74
,
35
,
62
]
. Non-convex online optimization
[
37
,
31
,
30
]
poses greater challenges than its convex counterparts
[
62
,
74
,
36
,
8
]
. Notable contributions in this field include adversarial multi-armed bandit algorithms
[
11
,
38
,
39
,
48
]
and the Follow-the-Perturbed-Leader approach
[
2
,
47
,
68
]
. Hazan et al.
[
37
]
introduced window-smoothed local regret for gradient averaging in non-convex models, which Hallak et al.
[
32
]
extended to non-smooth, non-convex problems. Inspired by their work, we employ local regret for OBO without window-smoothing.

Zeroth-Order Optimization.

Single-Level ZO Optimization has been widely studied in both offline
[
26
,
18
,
1
,
60
,
58
]
and online settings
[
53
,
30
,
31
,
72
,
6
]
. We next review closely related work. Liu et al.
[
53
]
proposed ZOO-ADMM, a gradient-free online optimization algorithm utilizing ADMM. Guan et al.
[
31
]
studied online non-convex optimization with limited oracle feedback. Research on online non-convex optimization with bandit feedback includes work by Heliou et al.
[
38
]
, which established bounds on global static and dynamic regret using dual averaging, further refined in
[
39
]
. Gao et al.
[
24
]
extended these ideas to ZO algorithms. Flaxman et al.
[
21
]
provided algorithms for bandit online optimization of convex functions using ZO gradient approximation. Our work closely relates to
[
65
]
, which proposes a Hessian-free method approximating the Jacobian matrix using a ZO method based on finite differences of gradients. In contrast, our method uses function oracles to approximate both the Hessian and gradients and is derivative-free. We also point out the recent
work
[
3
]
on ZO stochastic algorithms for solving bilevel problems when neither the upper/lower objective values nor their unbiased gradient estimates are available. Their approach, limited to the
offline
setting, does not include numerical results, thus leaving its practical efficiency unclear.

Appendix B
Additional Preliminaries and Notations

B.1
Preliminary Lemmas

We first provide several useful lemmas for the main proofs.

Definition B.1

(
Projected gradient

[
27
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
be a closed convex set. Then, the projected gradient for any
\alpha_{t}>0
and
{\bf{p}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
is defined as

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}\left({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}}\right):=\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}\left({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right),

where

\displaystyle{\bf{x}}^{+}=\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\left({\bf{x}}-\alpha_{t}{\bf{p}}\right),

(36)

and
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\left[\cdot\right]
denotes the orthogonal projection operator onto set
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma B.2
.

[
29
, Lemma 13]

If
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a
\mu_{f}
-strongly convex function with respect to some norm
\|\cdot\|
, and
{\bf{x}}^{*}
is the minimizer of
f
(i.e.
{\bf{x}}^{*}=\arg\min_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}f({\bf{x}})
), then we have
\forall\ {\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{f}}{2}\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{*}\|^{2}\leq f({\bf{x}})-f({\bf{x}}^{*})\leq\frac{1}{2\mu_{f}}\|\nabla f({\bf{x}})\|^{2}.

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose
f({\bf{x}})
is
L
-smooth, and
{\bf{x}}^{*}\in\text{argmin}_{{\bf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}f({\bf{x}})
. Then, we can upper bound the magnitude of the gradient at any given point
{\bf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in terms of the objective sub optimality at
{\bf{x}}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2L}\|\nabla f({\bf{x}})\|^{2}\leq f({\bf{x}})-f({\bf{x}}^{*})\leq\frac{L}{2}\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{*}\|^{2}.

(37)

Lemma B.4
.

For any
{\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following holds for any
c>0
:

\displaystyle{\|{\bf{x}}+{\bf{y}}\|}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+c){\|{\bf{x}}\|}^{2}+\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\right){\|{\bf{y}}\|}^{2}.

We also utilize a basic yet important property of the projected-gradient mapping.

Lemma B.5
.

[
27
, Proposition 1]

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})
denote the projected gradient as defined in Definition
B.1
. For any
{\bf{x}},{\bf{p}}_{1},{\bf{p}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\alpha_{t}>0
, it holds that

\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}}_{1})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}}_{2})\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert{\bf{p}}_{1}-{\bf{p}}_{2}\right\rVert.

Lemma B.6
.

[
37
, Proposition 2.4]

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})
denote the projected gradient as defined in Definition
B.1
. For any
{\bf{x}},{\bf{p}}_{1},{\bf{p}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\alpha_{t}>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}}_{1}+{\bf{p}}_{2})\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}}_{1})\right\rVert+\left\lVert{\bf{p}}_{2}\right\rVert.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})
be as given in Definition
B.1
.
Then, for any
{\bf{p}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\alpha_{t}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{\bf{p}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})\right\rangle\geq\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})\right\rVert^{2}.

Proof.

By the definition of
{\bf{x}}^{+}
, the optimality condition of (
36
) is

\displaystyle\left\langle{\bf{p}}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}}^{+}-{\bf{x}}),{\bf{z}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right\rangle\geq 0,\quad\forall{\bf{z}}\in\mathcal{X}.

Letting
{\bf{z}}={\bf{x}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{\bf{p}},{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}\left\langle{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+},{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right\rangle,

which can be rearranged to

\displaystyle\left\langle{\bf{p}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}\left\langle{\bf{p}},{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha_{t}^{2}}\left\langle{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+},{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{+}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{X},\alpha_{t}}({\bf{x}};{\bf{p}})\right\rVert^{2}.

∎

B.2
Examples Illustrating Regularity Conditions

Theorem
2.6
achieves sublinear bilevel regret when the variations
V_{T}
and
H_{2,T}
are
o(T^{2/3})
and
o(T^{1/3})
, respectively. Below, we provide some examples of online optimization in both single-level and bilevel settings to illustrate when this occurs.

Example B.8
.

Consider function
f_{t}({\bf{x}})=\|{\bf{A}}_{t}{\bf{x}}-{\bf{b}}_{t}\|^{2}
, where
{\bf{A}}_{t}=[1,0;0,1+\frac{1}{t}]
,
{\bf{b}}_{t}=(1,1)
. It follows from (
11
) that
V_{T}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\max_{{\bf{x}}}|f_{t}({\bf{x}})-f_{t-1}({\bf{x}})|=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\right)^{2}|
, and

\displaystyle V_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\left(\frac{1}{t}-\frac{1}{t-1}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{t}+\frac{1}{t-1}\right)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\left(\frac{t-1-t}{t(t-1)}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{t}+\frac{1}{t-1}\right)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\left(-\frac{1}{t(t-1)}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{t}+\frac{1}{t-1}\right)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\frac{1}{t(t-1)}||\frac{t-1+t}{t(t-1)}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|\frac{2}{t(t-1)^{2}}|.

Then,

V_{T}\leq\sum_{t=2}^{T}\frac{2}{t^{3}}\approx\int_{2}^{T}\frac{2}{t^{3}}dt=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{T^{2}}
. As
T\rightarrow\infty
,
V_{T}
becomes bounded and approaches a constant value, indicating that
V_{T}
grows slower than
T
itself.

Example B.9
.

Let

f_{t}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{cases}\left(-\frac{1}{T},0\right)&\text{if }t\text{ is even};\\
\left(0,-\frac{1}{T}\right)&\text{if }t\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}

Then,
V_{T}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\max_{\mathbf{x}}|f_{t}(\mathbf{x})-f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x})|=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Example B.10
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{X}=\left[-1,1\right]\subset\mathbb{R}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
, and consider a sequence of quadratic cost functions

\displaystyle f_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(x+2{a}_{t}^{(1)}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left({y}-a_{t}^{(2)}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle g_{t}({x},{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{y}^{2}-\left({x}-a_{t}^{(2)}\right){y},

where
a_{t}^{(1)}=1/t
and
a_{t}^{(2)}=1/\sqrt{t}
for all
t\in[T]
.

We have

\displaystyle{y}^{*}_{t}({x})={x}-a_{t}^{(2)},

and

\displaystyle\quad f_{t}(x,y^{*}_{t}(x))-f_{t-1}(x,y^{*}_{t-1}(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(x+2{a}_{t}^{(1)}\right)^{2}-\left(x+2{a}_{t-1}^{(1)}\right)^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left({y}^{*}_{t}({x})-a_{t}^{(2)}\right)^{2}-\left({y}^{*}_{t-1}({x})-a_{t-1}^{(2)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(x^{2}+4x{a}_{t}^{(1)}+4({a}_{t}^{(1)})^{2}\right)-\left(x^{2}+4x{a}_{t-1}^{(1)}+4({a}_{t-1}^{(1)})^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(({x}-a_{t}^{(2)})^{2}-2({x}-a_{t}^{(2)})a_{t}^{(2)}+(a_{t}^{(2)})^{2}\right)-\left(({x}-a_{t-1}^{(2)})^{2}-2({x}-a_{t-1}^{(2)})a_{t-1}^{(2)}+(a_{t-1}^{(2)})^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2x\left({a}_{t}^{(1)}-{a}_{t-1}^{(1)}-{a}_{t}^{(2)}+{a}_{t-1}^{(2)}\right)+2\left(({a}_{t}^{(1)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(1)})^{2}+({a}_{t}^{(2)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(2)})^{2}\right).

Taking the maximum over
x
and using
x\in[-1,1]:

\displaystyle\sup_{x}|f_{t}(x,y^{*}_{t}(x))-f_{t-1}(x,y^{*}_{t-1}(x))|

\displaystyle=2\left|{a}_{t}^{(1)}-{a}_{t-1}^{(1)}\right|+2\left|-{a}_{t}^{(2)}+{a}_{t-1}^{(2)}\right|

\displaystyle+2\left|({a}_{t}^{(1)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(1)})^{2}\right|+2\left|({a}_{t}^{(2)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(2)})^{2}\right|.

Since
a_{t}^{(1)}=1/t
and
a_{t}^{(2)}=1/\sqrt{t}
for all
t\in[T]
, then we have

\displaystyle|{a}_{t}^{(1)}-{a}_{t-1}^{(1)}|\approx\frac{1}{t^{2}},\quad|{a}_{t}^{(2)}-{a}_{t-1}^{(2)}|\approx\frac{1}{2t^{3/2}},

\displaystyle|({a}_{t}^{(1)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(1)})^{2}|\approx\frac{1}{t^{3}},\quad|({a}_{t}^{(2)})^{2}-({a}_{t-1}^{(2)})^{2}|\approx\frac{1}{t^{2}}.

Then, we get

\displaystyle V_{T}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{x}|f_{t}(x,y^{*}_{t}(x))-f_{t-1}(x,y^{*}_{t-1}(x))|=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(\frac{2}{t^{2}}+\frac{1}{2t^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{t^{3}}\right).

The series
\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(\frac{2}{t^{2}}+\frac{1}{2t^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{t^{3}}\right)
converges, implying
V_{T}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle H_{2,T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{x}\|y^{*}_{t}(x)-y^{*}_{t-1}(x)\|^{2}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{x}\|{x}-a_{t}^{(2)}-{x}+a_{t-1}^{(2)}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|-a_{t}^{(2)}+a_{t-1}^{(2)}|^{2}=\sum_{t=2}^{T}|{a}_{t}^{(2)}-{a}_{t-1}^{(2)}|^{2}\approx\sum_{t=2}^{T}\frac{1}{4t^{3}},

which implies
H_{2,T}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

To achieve
V_{T}=o(T^{2/3})
and
H_{2,T}=o(T^{1/3})
, the changes in the cost functions
f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))
and
{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})
should decay to zero faster than
\mathcal{O}(1/t^{a})
with
a>1/3
. For example, if the coefficients in the functions change as
\mathcal{O}(1/t^{a})
with
a>1/3
, then the cumulative sum over
T
will be
o(T^{2/3})
.
When
f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))
and
{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})
decay as
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{t})
, then the total variation grows at most as
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
.

Appendix C
Proof of Regret Bounds for Simultaneous Online Gradient Descent (SOGD)

Proof Roadmap
.
We introduce Lemma
C.2
, which
quantifies the error between the approximated direction of the momentum-based gradient estimator,
{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}
, and the true direction,
{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})
, at each
iteration.
To bound the error of the lower-level variable, we provide Lemma
C.4
, which captures the gap
\|{\bf{y}}_{t+1}-{{\bf{y}}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|^{2}
and incorporates the error introduced in Lemma
C.2
.
Moreover, we provide Lemma
C.6
, which quantifies the error between the approximated
direction of the momentum-based gradient estimator,

{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}
, and the true direction,
\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t}\right){\bf{v}}_{t}+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})
, at each iteration.
To bound the error of the system solution, we provide Lemma
C.8
, which captures the gap
\left\lVert{\bf{v}}_{t+1}-{{\bf{v}}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right\rVert^{2}
and incorporates the error introduced
in Lemma
C.6
.
Moreover, we provide Lemma
C.9
, which quantifies the error between the approximated
direction of the momentum-based hypergradient estimator,

{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{x}}
, and the true direction,
\nabla_{{\bf{x}}}f_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})+\nabla_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{z}}_{t}\right){\bf{v}}_{t}
, at each iteration.
We also present Lemma
C.11
, which provides an upper bound for the projection mapping and relates to the three errors discussed in Lemmas
C.4
,
C.8
, and
C.9
. Finally, by combining these lemmas and appropriately setting the parameters, we achieve the desired result.

C.1
Proof of Lemma
2.1

Proof.

By letting
\nu=1-\eta
for
\eta\in(0,1)
, the window-smoothed
gradient

\displaystyle{\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}\nu^{i}{\nabla}f_{t-i}({\bf{x}}_{t-i},{\bf{y}}_{t-i};\mathcal{B}_{t-i}),

is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{j=t-w+1}^{t}(1-\eta)^{t-j}{\nabla}f_{j}({\bf{x}}_{j},{\bf{y}}_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j}).

(38)

Let
{{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{x}}}={\nabla}F_{t,\nu}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})
. Then (
38
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle{{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{W}{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+\frac{1}{W}\sum_{j=t-w+1}^{t-1}(1-\eta)^{t-j}{\nabla}f_{j}({\bf{x}}_{j},{\bf{y}}_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j}).

Since

\displaystyle(1-\eta){{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\eta)}{W}\sum_{j=t-w}^{t-1}(1-\eta)^{t-1-j}{\nabla}f_{j}({\bf{x}}_{j},{\bf{y}}_{j};\mathcal{B}_{j}),

we have

\displaystyle{{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{W}{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-\eta){{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}-\frac{(1-\eta)^{w}}{W}{\nabla}f_{t-w}({\bf{x}}_{t-w},{\bf{y}}_{t-w};\mathcal{B}_{t-w}),

with
f_{i}(\cdot)=0
for all
i\leq 0
.

If

w=t
and
W=\frac{1}{\eta}
then, we have

\displaystyle{{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\eta{\nabla}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\mathcal{B}_{t})+(1-\eta){{\bf{d}}}^{{\bf{x}}}_{t-1}.

∎

C.2
Bounds on the Inner Decision Variable

In the following, inspired by offline BO
[
71
,
16
]
and OBO
[
69
,
51
]
, we provide a set of lemmas for the analysis of SOGD. We first present a lemma that characterizes the Lipschitz continuity of the approximate gradients, as well as the inner and system solutions.

Lemma C.1
.

Under Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
, for all
{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, and the search directions
\{{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{x}}\}_{t=1}^{T}
and
\{{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}\}_{t=1}^{T}
generated by Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{x}}-\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\right\|^{2}\leq M_{f}^{2}\left(\left\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\left\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right),

(39a)

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}\right\|^{2}\leq M_{{\bf{v}}}^{2}\left(\left\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\left\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right),

(39b)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}))-\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}^{\prime},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}^{\prime}))\right\|\leq L_{f}\left\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\right\|,

(39c)

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}^{\prime})\right\|\leq L_{{\bf{y}}}\left\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\right\|,

(39d)

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}})-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}^{\prime})\right\|\leq L_{{\bf{v}}}\left\|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\right\|,

(39e)

where
M_{f}
,
M_{{\bf{v}}}
, and
(L_{{\bf{y}}},L_{{\bf{v}}},L_{f})
are defined in (
42
), (
43
), and (
44
), respectively.

Proof.

We first show (
39a
).

Using Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
, we have
\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)\succeq\mu_{g}
, and

\displaystyle\|{\bf{v}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|=\|\left(\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)\|\leq\frac{\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}.

(40)

Observe that

\displaystyle\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{x}}-\nabla f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\bf{x}}{f}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{\bf{x}}{f}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\|

\displaystyle+\|{\bf{v}}_{t}\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\bf{x}}{f}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{\bf{x}}{f}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\|

\displaystyle+\|\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|

\displaystyle+\|{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|\|\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{x}}{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\ell_{f,1}+\frac{{\ell}_{g,2}\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}\right)\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\ell_{g,1}\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq M_{f}^{2}\left(\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|\right),

(41)

where

M_{f}:=\sqrt{2}\max\left\{\ell_{f,1}+\frac{{\ell}_{g,2}\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}},\ell_{g,1}\right\},

(42)

the third inequality is by Assumption
2.3
, and the last inequality follows from (
40
).

Next, we establish (
39b
).

Since
{{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}}^{*}:=\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))+\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}^{2}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right){\bf{v}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})=0
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}\|

\displaystyle=\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}-{{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{v}}}}^{*}\|

\displaystyle=\|{\bf{v}}_{t}\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})+\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})

\displaystyle-\left({\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}\left({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)+\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\right)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\left(\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\right){\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|

\displaystyle+\|\nabla^{2}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\left({\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\right)\|

\displaystyle+\|\nabla_{\bf{y}}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})-\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}f_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t}))\|.

Then, from Assumption
2.3
and (
40
), we have

\displaystyle\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{\bf{v}}\|

\displaystyle\leq{\ell}_{g,2}\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|\|{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\ell_{g,1}\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\ell_{f,1}\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{{\ell}_{g,2}\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}+\ell_{f,1}\right)\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\ell_{g,1}\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq M_{{\bf{v}}}\left(\|{\bf{y}}_{t}-{\bf{y}}_{t}^{*}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|+\|{\bf{v}}_{t}-{\bf{v}}^{*}_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t})\|\right),

where

M_{{\bf{v}}}:=\sqrt{2}\max\left\{\frac{{\ell}_{g,2}\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}+\ell_{f,1},\ell_{g,1}\right\}.

(43)

The proofs of Eqs. (
39c
)-(
39e
) follow from
[
69
, Lemma 17]
by setting

\displaystyle L_{{\bf{y}}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\ell_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}},

(44)

\displaystyle L_{{\bf{v}}}

\displaystyle:=\ell_{f,1}+\frac{\ell_{g,1}\ell_{f,1}}{\mu_{g}}+\frac{\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}\left(\ell_{g,2}+\frac{\ell_{g,1}{\ell_{g,2}}}{\mu_{g}}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle L_{f}

\displaystyle:=\ell_{f,1}+\frac{\ell_{g,1}(\ell_{f,1}+M_{f})}{\mu_{g}}+\frac{\ell_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}}\left(\ell_{g,2}+\frac{\ell_{g,1}{\ell_{g,2}}}{\mu_{g}}\right),

where the other constants are defined in Assumption
2.3
.
∎

The following lemma is inspired by
[
71
]
and can be viewed as an extension of
[
71
]
to the online setting.

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
B3.
and
C1.
hold.
Let
\{({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t},{\bf{v}}_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
be generated according to Algorithm

1
.
For
e_{t}^{g}
defined as

\displaystyle e_{t}^{g}:={{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t}),

(45)

we have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}(1+48\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}){\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}+24(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+48(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}.

(46)

Proof.

From Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle{\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})+(1-\gamma_{t+1})({\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t}-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})).

Then, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\|{{\bf{d}}}_{t+1}^{{\bf{y}}}-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})+(1-\gamma_{t+1})({\bf{d}}^{{\bf{y}}}_{t}-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1}))-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\|(1-\gamma_{t+1})e_{t}^{g}+({\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1}))

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\gamma_{t+1})\left({\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\right)-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})\|^{2},

which implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\|({\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1}))

\displaystyle-(1-\gamma_{t+1})\left({\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\right)-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})

\displaystyle-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})+{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle+2(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})

\displaystyle-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})+{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})\|^{2},

where the second inequality follows from Cauchy–Schwartz inequality and Assumption
C1.
.

Moreover, from Cauchy–Schwartz inequality, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\|^{2}.

From Assumption
B3.
, we have

\displaystyle\quad{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}+2{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{y}}_{t+1}-{\bf{y}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}\|^{2},

and

\displaystyle\quad{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t+1};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{x}}_{t+1},{\bf{y}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t};\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{y}}_{t+1}-{\bf{y}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}\|^{2}.

From the two inequalities above, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+24(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}\left({\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}+\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{d}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}\|^{2}\right).

Since
e_{t}^{g}:={{\bf{d}}}_{t}^{{\bf{y}}}-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t+1}^{g}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}+24(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+48(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+48(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}(1+48\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}){\mathbb{E}}\|e_{t}^{g}\|^{2}+2\gamma_{t+1}^{2}\frac{\sigma_{g_{{\bf{y}}}}^{2}}{\bar{b}}+24(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\bf{x}}_{t+1}-{\bf{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+6(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})-{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t+1}({\bf{z}}_{t+1})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+48(1-\gamma_{t+1})^{2}\ell_{g,1}^{2}\beta_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\nabla}_{{\bf{y}}}g_{t}({\bf{x}}_{t},{\bf{y}}_{t})\|^{2}.

∎

Lemma C.3
.
